TECHNICAL DISCUSSION
A t t h e o u t s e t a l i t e r a t u r e s e a r c h f a i l e d t o uncover previous

t h e o r e t i c a l o r experimental work that could be used d i r e c t l y t o design
t h e bubble-lift-pump,

Most o f t h e previous work on bubble-lift-pumps

has been concerned with a i r bubbles used t o pump water i n very l o n g
tubes,

A t y p i c a l example is t h e work o f Nicklin (Reference 2).

The

a i r l i f t pump theory o f Nicklin does n o t consider heat nor mass t r a n s f e r
between t h e bubble and t h e l i q u i d ,

Since a bubble-lift-pump

used with

l i q u i d hydrogen o r l i q u i d oxygen could involve considerable heat t r a n s f e r
(depending on t h e gas i n j e c t i o n temperature), and a considerable amount
of mass t r a n s f e r due t o t h e high l i q u i d vapor p r e s s u r e , t h e s e f a c t o r s
cannot be ignored,
Another f a c t o r that precludes d i r e c t a p p l i c a t i o n of N i c k l i n ' s
work i n t h e design o f a bubble-lift-pump

for t h i s particular application

Abstract:
The r e s u l t s of a study on t h e e f f e c t of b a f f l e s on n a t u r a l convection flow
and on s t r a t i f i c a t i o n a r e 'presented. T h i s study, which i s expected t o be
ap2licable t o cryogenics, w a s performed w i t h water r a t h e r than a cryogen
i n a two-dimensional v e s s e l t o f a c i l i t a t e o p t i c a l methods f o r determining
t h e nature of t h e flow. Visudl s t u d i e s using a Schlieren apparatus and
temperature measurements using thermocouples were performed. Two geometries:
a v e s s e l w i t h only heated v e r t i c a l walls and a v e s s e l w i t h a heated v e r t i c a i
wall and a heated i n c l i n e d bottom, were considered.

.

Baffles on heated v e r t i c a l w a l l s were only p a r t i a l l y e f f e c t i v e i n reducing'
s t r a t i f i c a t i o n . The e f f e c t i v e n e s s decreases w i t h t i m e and increases w i t h
t h e n m b e r and s i z e of b a f f l e s . The Schlieren s t u d i e s showed that i n i t i a l l y
the b a f f l e s e f f e c t i v e l y d i v e r t t h e n a t u r a l convection flow b u t l a t e r t h e
Flow r e a t t a c h e s t o t h e w a l l above t h e b a f f l e making t h e . b a f f l e i n e f f e c t i v e .
I n the i n c l i n e d plake s t u d i e s s i g n i f i c a n t s t r a t i f i c a t i o n r e s u l t s only when
t h e r a t i o of bottom t o wall heat flux i s l e s s than o r equal t o 1, b u t when
t h i s r a t i o i s 2 or g r e a t e r there is l i t k l e s t r a t i f i c a t i o n . Schlieren s t u d i e s
i n d i c a t e t h a t b a f f l e s on the bottom have a pronounced e f f e c t on t h e n a t u r a l
convection flow b u t t h e r e i s l i t t l e e f f e c t on t h e ultimate s t r a t i f i c a t i o n .

INTRODUCTION
The thermal energy absorption by l i q u i d prope1lan-h of space vehicles r e s u l t s
i n a weight p e n a l t y which occurs from eithe+ a l o s s of vaporized p r o p e l l a n t
or an increase i n tank w e i g h t required t o contain t h e warmer propellant. The
of
magnitude of t h e l a t t e r penalty increases with the s e ~ e r i t y t h e l i q u i d
propellant temperature v a r i a t i o n . Various methods f o r p r e d i c t i n g s t r a t i f i c a t i o n have been developed ( R e f s . 1 & 2 ) and it i s a l o g i c a l next s t e p t o examine
and evaluate methods of reducing s t r a t i f i c a t i o n .
Various methods, both a c t i v e and passive, have been proposed t o reduce s t r a t i f i c a t i o n and one of these, a passive method, i s the use of a n t i - s t r a t i f i c a t i o n
b a f f l e s . A experimental study has been conducted t o determine t h e e f f e c t of
n
b a f f l e s on n a t u r a l convection flow and on s t r a t i f i c a t i o n . These s t u d i e s include
t h e use of t h e Schlieren and shadowgraph techniques t o v i s u a l i z e and a s s i s t i n
i n t e r p r e t i n g t h e n a t u r a l convection flow, and a l s o a c t u a l temperature measurement using thermocouples t o determine t h e magnitude of s t r a t i f i c a t i o n . These
s t u d i e s were performed i n a smal1,twpdimensional l a b o r a t o r y s c a l e apparatus
using water a s t h e t e s t f l u i d . It w a s not t h e i n t e n t nor was it possible t o
s c a l e the f l u i d dynamics of t h e s e t e s t s t o the t-DicallJf l a r g e space v e s s e l s
containing' cryogenic f l u i d s b u t r a t h e r t o 'gain a g r e a t e r understanding of t h e
e f f e c t s of b a f f l e s w i t h t h e i n t e n t i o n that t h e y m y l a t e r be applied t o .a l a r g e
space v e s s e l i f proved a t t r a c t i v e . The use of water, although not a cryogenic
f l u i d , I n t h e small two-dimensional v e s s e l permitted numerous geometry and t e s t
conditions t o be i n v e s t i g a t e d e a s i l y .

has an inherent t r a n s i e n t a s s o c i a t e d with it. It i s f e l t , however, that t h e

INTRODUCTION

This r e p o r t presents t h e i n i t - i a l r e s u l t s of a program of research d e a l ing with t r a n s i e n t , f r e e convection i n s i d e closed containers,

The problem

reported i s t h e first phase of a program d i r e c t e d a t a n understanding and
p r e d i c t i o n of f l u i d behavior i n closed containers s u b j e c t t o various disturbances, including those of ambient heat flux, change i n w a l l temperature,
p r e s s u r i z a t i o n , change i n g r a v i t a t i o n a l f i e l d , and l i q u i d discharge.

The

o b j e c t i v e s of t h e general program a r e t h e p r e d i c u o n of t r a n s i e n t v e l o c i t y
p r o f i i e s , temperature s t r a t i f i c a t i o n , and pressure h i s t o r i e s i n such containers

-

The present r e p o r t treats l a m i n a r t r a n s i e n t free convection i n a two-

-

dimensional container having a liquid-vapor ,-interface

Transient processes

a r e introduced by a sudden increase i n t h e temperature o f ’ t h e container w a l l .

and by a sudden a p p l i c a t i o n of h e a t flux t o t h e e x t e r n a l surfaces of t h e container.

For a systen: a t constant pressure and a t n o m 1 g r a v i t y t h e result-

ing t r a n s i e n t flow p a t t e r n s and temperature s t r a t i f i c a t i o n a r e computed,
The problem i s formulated from t h e complete Navier-Stokes equations
coupled w i t h those from t h e F i r s t Law of Thermodynamics and t h e Conservation
of Mass.

Boundary-layer approximations are not made s i n c e t h e geometry and

boundary conditions i n v a l i d a t e complete boundary-layer flow and r e q u i r e addit i o n a l momentum considerations not u s u a l l y found i n boundary-layer c a l c u l a t i o n s .
Reduction of t h e governing equations i s done by numerical procedures using

Combined with t h e experimental program p r e s e n t l y under way, t h e s e re-

sults w i l l a i d i n t h e establishment of c r i t e r i a

IV. FESULTS
Calculations have been carried out for the case of a container with a n
insulated bottom whose w a l l s are subjected t o a uniform heat flux, and the
liquid surface i s maintained e i t h e r a t t h e i n i t i a l temperature To, a t a t e m perature, T,,

higher than To, or i s adiabatic.

The last case is expected t o

approximate the transient convection i n the liquid when exposed t o a nonpressurized gas.

Different levels of heat f l u x were considered, including

10 and 1000 Btu/hr/ft2.

For these calculations, t h e f l u i d properties chosen were those of liquid
nitrogen i n i t i a l l y a t atmospheric pressure, the i n i t i a l saturation temperat u r e w a s 140"R.

The f l u i d properties were evaluated a t a temperature &qual

t o the average of the i n i t i a l temperature and the l i q u i d surface temperakure.

i n Table I .

CONCLUSIONS

The equations describing the t r a n s i e n t , two-dimensional, l d n a r , natural
convection i n a rectangular closed container having a vapor-liquid i n t e r f a c e
have been solved using e x p l i c i t f i n i t e - d i f f e r e n c e approximations.

The velocity

and temperature d i s t r i b u t i o n s indicate t h a t , f o r small time periods, t h e flow
i s of a boundary-layer type, except near t h e bottom and the liquid-vapor i n t e r -

face.

No indications of numerical i n s t a b i l i t y were encountered.

The s i z e of

t h e time increment was r e s t r i c t e d t o small values by s t a b i l i t y considerations
based on t h e method of Von Neuaann.

However, a small t i m e increment i s de-

s i r a b l e i n order t o obtain accurate t r a n s i e n t r e s u l t s a t small time.

On the

o t h k hand, i f steady s t a t e r e s u l t s a r e desired, t h e number of computations
using the e x p l i c i t method will be large f o r f i n e g r i d s and f o r systems having

a high heat f l u .

The machine time may be of t h e order of 2 hours i n t h i s

-In

case,

LC

and t h e use of i m p l i c i t methods would be s q e r i o r .

However, t h e ap-

p l i c a t i o n of implicit-difference methods t o t h i s problem and t o problems w i t h
other geometry i s being continued.
The r e s u l t s obtained using the present method agree q u a l i t a t i v e l y w i t h
related cases reported i n the l i t e r a t u r e .

A HYPOTHESIS FOR HELIUM ADSORPTION/DESORPTION
IN SLUSH HYDROGEN
by
Clarence A. Schalla
Lockheed Missiles and Space Company
Sunriyvale; California

Helium solubility in l i q a d hydrogen at subcoolcd temperatures and at

normal pressures is extremely low, being proportional to liquid tenrperature.

Helium dilution in slush (liquid-solid) hydrogen, on the other

hand, can be expected to be appreciable, There is no infomation available on.the adsorption of helium by gas-soLid interfaces of the solids
in slusn hydrogen.
it.

This paper introduces an initial hypothesis concerning

A l s o introiluced is the hypothesis of helium desorption cooling of

slush hydrogen.

The latter, if observed, would be the antithesis tc the

hydrogen stratification phenomena, Accordingly, slush germination via
desorption cooling under supercooled conditions of storage ia here
postulated to anticipate triple-point anomalies in future exploratory
investigations with slush hydrogen.

That is, such data may very well

show variances with the normally slnguhr triGle-point of hydlrogen.
The concern over the solubility of helium in liquid hydrogen has been
not unlike that of nitrogen solubility in liqdd oxygen.

Both of these

gaseous absorbates are commonly used as purgants and pressurants. Any
solubility carryover and evolution would somewhat handicap prcpellant
utilization (because of early onset of cavitation).

Accordingly,

propellant dilution with these inert gases does precipitate apprehensions over nozzle f l o w control and metering systems sensitivity.
Helium solubility in saturated and in subcooled liquid hydrogen is
considered to be low for the normal range of pressures concerned.

Appreciable a d s o r p t i v i t y conceivably

exists a t t h e surface of t h e hydrogen s o l i d s .

Hydrogen aoUda appear t o be c r y s t a l l i n e and they a r e conaidcmd t o have

a sharp melting p o i n t r a t h e r than a softening range.

Therefore, such

solids have msny i n t e r f a c e s , and physical adsorption is t o be expected.

DESORPTION KYPOTKESIS
Desorption or helium evolution ia the converse to the solubility and
adsorption processes.

In an isentropic expansion, such as m y occur
a

during depressurization of hydrogen storage in Epace, the adsorbed

_helium is presumed to desorb as shown in Figure

4
.

During such desorp-

tion conditions in hydrogen venting, the evolved helium gas is likely
,

,’
/

/

to induce Joule-Thornson cooling of the hydrogen involved.

presents this mechanism, which can occur in a temperature regime w e l l
below the helium inversion temperature.

194

Figure 5

It is here p o s t u l a t e d that when Joule-Thomaon cooling of helium does
develop during a desorption process, a supercooled s l u s h hydrogen
condition would occur wherever subcooled hydrogen conditions exist.
Accordingly, s l u s h hydrogen would germinate a t these supercooled
points, t o be d e t e c t e d a t variance with t h e c h a r a c t e r i s t i c themo-

dynamic conditions of t h e t r i p l e point s t a t e .

Such could be considered

t h e a n t i t h e s i s t o the hydrogen s t r a t i f i c a t i o n phenomenon.
A n y such hydrogen slush anomalies and thermodynamic v a r i a t i o n s from

helium aasorption could handicap s l u s h production and propellant
u t i l i z a t i o n control.

With these adsorp%ion/desoqtion hypotheses it i s implied that explor*
'

a t o r y work w i l l reveal anomalies and phenomena somewhat'detrimental t o
the space use of slush hydrogen.

However, this may be construed, it is

apparent that research e f f o r t and implemental instrumentation development

is overdue i n work a t and around the t r i p l e p o i n t of hydrogen.

Because

helium is a prominent adjunct t o the handling and storage of s l u s h hydrogen, it also appears that such concerted research and development be

directed t o f l i g h t - t y p e sensing instnunentation and controls.
I n formulating t h e hy-gotheses of this paper, various thermodynamic
processes were probed with pseudo data and quasi conditions,

This *as

p r i n c i p a l l y f o r t h e purpose of presenting a realistic approach i n applying research logic i n what can be used i n a p l a n to define exploratory

t e a t s and instrumentation requirement%.

ABSTRACT

detecting-a d i f f e r e n c e i n magnitude of t h e property between the vapor
and liquid p h a s e s .

The transducer respectively converts the p r e s e n c e

of v a p o r o r liquid i n t o a n output s i g n a l which is b i n a r y (i. e. e i t h e r on
o r off).

(vapor o r liquid) can p e r m i t a c c u r a t e l o c a t i o n of t h e c r y o g e n i c f l u i d

int e r f a c e.

p r o p e r t i e s of the v a p o r and liquid p h a s e of a c r y o g e n .

liquids n i t r o g e n and hydrogen.
Selecting t h e m o s t s u i t a b l e t r a n s d u c e r f o r a n a p p l i c a t i o n f r o m
those a v a i l a b l e r e q u i r e s knowledge of t h e u s e e n v i r o n m e n t .

ANALYTICAL AND EXPERIMENTAL STUDY OF
STRATIFICATION IN STANDARD AND REDUCED
GRAVITY FIELDS
by
Harper, S. E. Hurd, A. M. Levy, an$. D. M, T e l l e p
'Lockheed Missiles and Space Company
. ..
Sunnyvale, California

.

'

2 07-

ANALYTICAL AND EXPERIMENTAL STUDY OF STRATIFICATION
IN STANDARD AND REDUCED GRAVITY FIELDS

E . Y . Harper, S.E. Hurd, A.M. Levy, D.M. Tellep
ABSTRACT

/3=3
Temperature stratification of a subcooled fluid in a cylindrical tank subjected to a uniform
sidewall flux is studied both analytically and experimentally.

Using an integral technfque,

a closed form solution for the stratified layer growth and liquid temperature rise is
obtained for both the laminar and turbulent boundary layer flow regimes.

The solution

is based on the assumption of time-invariant dimensionless temperature profiles in the
stratified layer before the layer reaches the tank bottom. The solution identifies the
significant dimensionless parameters which govern stratification.
.. .
. .

..

Stratification experiments w e r e carried out in three different vessels with s i x different
fluids. The experiments demonstrated the utility of noncryogenic experiments in the
study of propellant stratification.

The tests covered a modified Grashof number range of

8
10 to 10 l4 and thereby simulated conditions in large cryogenic tanks over a wide range

of gravity levels and heat flux conditions.

The experiments demonstrated that the

dimensionless temperature profiles in the stratified layers w e r e time-invariant over the
complete range of Grashof numbers tested. The shape of the dimensionless temperature
profile determines a parameter defined as the energy integral which is an empirical
t e r m in the analysis. The energy integral w a s determined over a wide range of modified
8
14
Grashof numbers covering six orders of magnitude (10 to 10 ) and was found to be independent of the Prandtl Number.

Y

2 08

. A correlation has also been developed which extends the stratification predictions to
times after the stratified layer reaches the tank bottom.

INTRODUCTION
Stratification is defined as the development of temperature gradients in a contained fluid
a s a result of external heating. Stratification is assumed to occur because a free-

f
convection boundary layer carries the heated fluid at the wall to the top o the tank,
forming a growing layer of stratified liquid which is a t a higher temperature ihan Lhe
bulk.

This phenonienon is of particular importance in cryogenic propellant tanks, and it ma:.
have a significant effect on vehicle design and operation. In particular, the design of
venting devices, pressurization systems, insulation s y s t e m s , propellant feed pumps.
and tank Structures may be substantially influenced by propellant stratification. In order

.to ensure reliable designs, and t o avoid excessive penalties due to conservatism in
0

.

~.

prediction methods, a sound analytical model and experimental data applicable to the
. ..

conditions of interest are required.
In the present study, stratification of a subcooled fluid in a cylindrical tank subjected
to a uniform sidewall heat flux is considered.

Since the wall boundary layer is assumed

to be the primary mechanism for propellant stratification, a review of the flow regimes

for free-convection boundary layers will be given, and results will be presented to
indicate the range of boundary layer parameters characteristic of cryogenic propellants
in standard and reduced gravity fields. An analysis will then be outlined which utilizes
an integral technique to obtain a. solution for the development of the stratified layer and

21 3

for the associated liquid temperature rise. Finally, the results of stratification experiments conducted in noncryogenic fluids will be presented and compared to the analytical
model developed.
FLOW REGIMES FOR FREE-CONVECTION
BOUNDARY LAYERS
ONSET O F CONVECTIVE MOTION
The pi-ohlem of the onset of convective motion for horizontal layers of fluid has been
studied extensively by Rayleigh

1

and others

2,3,4

.

These studies indicated that the

significant dimensionless parameter is the Rayleigh number given by

and that convective motion is established for values of the Rayleigh number greater than
1100 to 1700. The x appearing in t h e Rayleigh number is here the layer thickness.

For situztions in which the fluid is bounded by vertical surfaces: there has been no
detailed analytic o r experimental work to determine the onset of convective motion.
5
G r o b e r . e t al. . suggest that a critic31 value of the Rayleigh number of 1700 may be

assumed to mark the beginning of convective motion in a fluid bounded by vertical s u r faces. In this case, the
ness.

214

>
;

appearing in the Rayleigh number is again the layer thick-

6

For a vertical flat plate, it is possible to use the data obtained by Schmidt and Beckmann
to compute an approximate value of Rayleigh number when a laminar boundary-layer
structure can be discerned. The Rayleigh number is between 430 and 16,000. The x
appearing in the Rayleigh number is the distance from the leading edge of the plate.
BOUNDARY LAYER TRANSITION

A laminar free-convection boundary layer is established on vertical surfaces at a
relatively low Rayleigh number. This laminar boundary layer persists as the Rayleigh
number increases. until a t some critical value the flow becomes unstable and the
boundary layer becomes turbulent. A s with forced convection. transition f r o m laminar
to turbulent flows does not occur at a discrete condition but probably takes place over
a range of Rayleigh numbers.

The literature on free convection ’

9

suggests

lo9 :

9
number of 10 is most often quoted.
The Rayleigh number as given above incorporates the conventional Grashof number that

is defined on the basis of the temperature difference between the wall and the bulk fluid
In most applications of interest, the heat flux to the propellant is the known quantity
rather than the temperature difference. When the heat flux is known, the analyses of
Refs. 9 , 1 0 , and 11 indicate that the modified Grashof number Gr* is the proper
dimensionless parameter.

INTEGRAL ANALYSIS
The model used for the'analysis of stratification is shown in Fig. 2. A cylindrical tank

is assumed to accelerate along its longitudinal axis, with a uniform and time-independent
sidewall heat flux to the liquid.

F o r a subcooled liquid, the energy transfer a t the free

surface is negligible. In the stratified layer, the temperatures a r e independent of radial
and azimuthal location. Below the stratified layer and outside the boundary l a y e r , the
temperature is constant and equal to the initial bulk temperature of the liquid.
radius is considered to be large compared with the boundary layer thickness.

The tank

by a dimensionless temperature profile which is invariant with time

EXPERIMENTAL STUDIES
A s e r i e s of stratification experiments were conducted with severnl fluids in cylindrical

containers to obtain basic data and evaluate the analytical prediction method. Although
interest in the problem of stratification arises mainly in connection with cryogenic
propellant tanks, the phenomenon'of stratification is not unique to cryogenic liquids and

its study can profitably be pursued with other liquids once theoretical considerations are
available to establish the simulation laws.

223

The use of noncryogenic liquids for stratification experiments greatly reduces test c o s t s ,
makes possible greater flexibility in control of test conditions, and considerably simplifies instrumentation. It also makes possible the achievement of laminar free -convection
boundary l a y e r s , thus simulating stratification under reduced gravity conditions.

OBJECTIVES AND PLAN OF EXPERIMENTS
A primary objective of the present experiments was to determine the validity of the
assumption of time- invariant dimensionless temperature profiles in the stratified layer,
and to establish the magnitude and functional dependence o the energy integral on the
f
modified Grashof and Prandtl numbers.

In o r d e r to obtain stratification data corresponding to low gravity conditions in cryogenic
8
10
tanks, tests were conducted with modified Grashof numbers in the range from 10 to 10
Since the modified Grashof number is proportional to the tank he.ight to the fourth power,
these relatively small Grashof numbers were most easily obtained by using a sm'all
tank.

For this purpose, a tank was constructed from a block of wood with a 3-inch

diameter hole in the center. A s t r i p of 0.001-inch thick stainless steel shim stock was
fastened to the inside wall of the tank and w a s used as a resistance heater to supply a
uniform heat flux to the stratifying liquid.
An intermediate range of modified Grashof numbers, from 2 . 3 x 10"

to 1. 5 x 10

14

, as

well as-a Prandtl number range from 4 . 5 to 32, were covered using s i x different fluids
in

3

9 . 75-inch diameter tank.

This tank was made from a thin-walled (0.025 in )

cylinder and was placed in a radiant heating facility and uniformly heated with a concentric a r r a y of tungsten filament quartz lamps.

224

A third facility used to obtain stratification data w a s a 10.75-inch inside diameter
pressure tank which utilized a resistance heater like that in the 3 -inch diameter tank.
In this tank, data have been obtained for modified Grashof numbers ranging in excess of10 l4 hy actively pressurizing the liquid, thereby subcooling it.

Large heat fluxes, with

correspondingly high wall temperatures, were then imposed without the occurrence of
nucleate boiling.

Another objective wxs to conduct tests w i t h a fluid which has a Prandtl

number close to one (that of liquid hydrogen). This was xccomplished by subcooling water

nt temperatures ranging to 370°F in the pressure tank. Also, in the pressure tank it was
possihle to run stmtification tests ivhich were immediately preceded b v a period wherein the fluid was simultaneously heated and vented near its saturation state. T h i s initial
venting procedure allowed the boundary layer and bulk flow fields to develop prior to
active pressurization and the initiation of strxtificntion. D a t a olkiincd from ihese tests
were used to evaluate the effect of transient flow field development upon the results
,

’

obtained from the. 3-inch and 9.7.5:inc.h diameter fanks.
The three test tanks described above had flat insulated bottoms. Thermocouples,
mounted on nonconducting probes, were used to measure fluid tempera tures along the
cylindrical axis of each tank.

The 9.75-inch diameter tank w a s also instrumented with

wall thermocouples in o r d e r to determine whether o r not boiling occurred and to check
the method used for calculating the wall temperature.

More details on the facilities

are given in Ref. 16.
All tests were conducted with aspect ratios (H/R) of 2.3.

225

E XPERKMENTA L RESULTS
A l l fluid properties used in the graphical presentation of results were evaluated using
the m a n mixed temperature of the fluid in the stratified layer.

To be consistent with

the model used f o r analysis, time w a s measured from the instant the surface thermocouple first sensed a temperature rise. Heat fluxes were determined by graphically
integrating the temperature profiles. Figure 3 shows some temperature profiles which

are t-ypical of the data obtained in all three tanks. In parts (a) and (b) of the figure, two
sets of profiles obtained in the pressure tank with water are shown. These two runs
indicate the significant change in temperature profile shape apparently caused by a
change in the modified Grashof and Prandtl numbers. Two sets of temperature profiles
obtained with trichloroethylene in the 3-inch and 9.75-inch tanks are given in parts (c)
and (d)'of the figure. A significant dependence of profile shape on Grashof number alone

is evident. The Prandtl number was the same in both tests. These general relationships appear to be independent of the fluid o r tank used.

It was necessary to calculate wall temperatures f o r the 3-inch diameter tank and the

layer analyses of Refs. 9 and 10 were used to calculate the temperature difference across

the boundary l a y e r at vertical locations corresponding to the axial thermocouples. The
wall temperature profile was then determined by adding the boundary layer temperature
difference to the corresponding measured bulk temperature. The accuracy of this method
was confirmed by wall temperature measurements obtained in the 9.75-inch diameter
tank. All the results presented in this paper correspond to times before any point on the
tank w a l l reached the liquid saturation temperature.

CONCLUSIONS
The closed-form solution, based on an integral analysis for predicting stratification of
subcooled fluids in cylindrical containers with uniform sidewall heating identified the
b a s i c dimensionless parameters as: the modified Grashof number, the Prandtl number,
the tank aspect ratio, and a dimensionless time.

It thus was extremely useful in

planning the experiments.

-239

The experimental results verified a principal assumption made in the analysis that the
nondimensional temperature profiles in the growing stratified layer are time-invariant.
The nondimensional profile shape appears to be a weak function of Prandtl number and

a strong function of Grashof number for a given tank aspect ratio. Correlation of this
functional dependence allows explicit prediction of the liquid temperature rise. The
8
1
4
correlation has been made over a range of modified Grashof numbers from 10 to 10
for an aspect ratio H/R = 2.3.
The experimental results generally confirmed the stratification analysis in t e r m s of
the stratified layer growth and the liquid surface temperature rise.
The initial conditions that exist in a vented cryogenic tank were simulated by conducting
stratification tests in a p r e s s u r e vessel with initially saturated water at Prandtl numbers near one.

. The

results indicate that boundary layer and surface spreading transients

do not affect the shape of the temperature profiles.

For times after the stratified layer reaches the tank bottom, when the integral analysis
is no longer valid, the experimental data has been successfully correlated by an empirical
extens ion of the analysis
The fact that theoretical and experimental results a r e given in terms of dimensionless
parameters permits application to a wide variety of conditions.

It is significant to note

the difference between stratification of subcooled fluids under standard-gravity and
reduced-gravity conditions.

It w a s pointed out that, for cryogenic propellants, the

tank wall free-convection boundary layer will p e r s i s t under reduced gravity conditions
but that it may be laminar rather than turbulent.

F o r the same heat flux to the propel-

lant, the surface temperature r i s e will be faster under reduced gravity conditions than

240

.

.

under standard gravity conditions.

The reason is that the m a s s flux in the wall boundary

layer is reduced with a reduction in acceleration; hence, the incoming energy is contained
in a thinner, and therefore hotter, stratified layer.

This result indicates that stratifica-

tion data obtained at standard gravity do not in general apply to reduced gravity conditions

unless attention has been devoted to proper scaling of the test.
The present study of subcooled stratification in a cylindrical tank with sidewall heating

is being continued to determine the effect of tank aspect ratio and to consider combined
sidewall and bottom heating.

by E W Otto

ABSTRACT

The problems encountered in attempting to operate systems containing a free liquid-vapor interface in a weightless environment are summarized.

The literature reporting the research applicable to or directed

toward solutions for these problems is reviewed.

Results of the research

deflriing the configuration of the interface as a function of liquid prop-

ertes and'system geometry are discussed.

The results of experimental

studies of the dynamlc behavior of the interface in response to changes
in gravity level, to outflow disturbances, and to acceleration disturbances &
e

presented.

This. study places iarticular emphasis on determina-

tion of the scaling laxs.that permit predsetion of the interface.behavior
as a function of model size.

1 INTRODUCTIOK
,
The increasing sophistication of space vehicles and t h e i r missions
has subjected an increasing v a r i e t y of liquid-vapor systems containing a
f r e e i n t e r f a c e t o a new environment--that of zero gravity.

O the h r t h
n

these systems r e l y almost invariably on t h e body force created by t h e
Earth’s g r a v i t a t i o n a l f i e l d t o separate and locate t h e l i q u i d and vapor
i n a consistent predictable manner,

Thus, l i q u i d s s e t t l e t o t h e bottom

of tanks, vapors separate from l i q u i d s and can be vented off i f necessary
a t the high points i n systems, and f r e e conyection and buoyancy e f f e c t s .
a s s i s t ’ i n t h e t r a n s f e r of heat.

I n a zero-gravity or weightless environ-

ment, however, t h e g r a v i t a t i o n a l or acceleration body forces a r e absent,
vhich introduces a number of questions concerning the behavior of t h e
liquid, both s t a t i c and dynamic, and the e f f e c t on heat-transfer phenomena,

This paper presents a survey of t h e various problem areas i n t h e

liquid-vapor systems t o be used aboard spacecraft and a review o f t h e
l i t e r a t u r e reporting t h e research applicable t o o r directed toward t h e
solutions f o r t h e s e problems.

The r e s u l t s of experimental studies of t h e

behavior of t h e i n t e r f a c e i n response t o changes i n g r a v i t y level, t o outfl’ow disturbances, and t o acceleration disturbances with p a r t i c u l a r emphasis on t h e s c a l t w l a w s governing t h e phenomena are also presented,

Propellant Tank System

A schematic diagram of a t y p i c a l space vehicle propellant tank sys-'

tern is sham i n Fig, l ( a ) .

Main propellant tanks contain a large, free

liquid-vapor i n t e r f a c e t h a t i s subject t o a nmber of disturbances, such
as heat gain and acceleration perturbations, as shown,
The primary function of a propellant tarik system i s t o s t o r e t h e
liquid i n readiness for use by t h e engine.

Thus the behavior of t h e

i n t e r f a c e and t h e propellant i n response t o tne various disturbances will
be important t o t h e engine restart operation.

If these perturbations a r e

s u f f i c i e n t l y severe, they may move l i q u i d completely away from the area

of t h e pump i n l e t , thus making it necessary t o c o l l e c t t h e l i q u i d by ac-

- celeration

of t h e vehicle along t h e thrust axis befor'e attemp'ting t o re-

start t h e engine.

Even i f t h e l i q u i d i s over t h e i n l e t ' t h e r e i s t h e pko-

blem of a l i m i t e d quantity of l i q u i d being available t o t h e pmp due t o

the possible pull-through of tne l i q u i d immediately above t h e tank o u t l e t
and the consequent ingestion of excessive vapor.
The heat gained by t h e tank may n e c e s s i t a t e venting ciuring long
szorage conditions.

The required frequency of venting trill depend on t h e

e f f e c t of the zero-gravity environment on t h e heat-transfer mechanism.
The success of t h e venting operation w i l l depend on t h e behavior of the
l i q u i d i n response t o t.he disturbances,

It may be necessary t o c o l l e c t

the l i q u i d and vapor before each vent cycle i n t h e same manner as f o r t h e
engine s t a r t i n g operation.
m e perturbations of t h e a t t i t u d e control system, i f s u f f i c i e n t l y

severe or if in resonance with the natural frequency of the surface, may
cause liquid to move over the top of the tank and down the other side.
Aside from causing trouble with venting procedures and possibly'pumping
operations, this liquid circulation constitutes a shifting center of
gravity of the vehicle and may cause severe stability problems in the
attitude control system.
2.2

Space Power Systems

A schematic diagram of a typical space power system is shown in
Fig. l ( b ) .

Two free liquid-vapor interfaces normally occur in these sys-

tems, one in the evaporator and one in the condenser.

The behavior of

these interfaces is complicated by the addition of heat in the evaporator,
the removal of heat in the condenser, and the probable impact of droplets
at high velocity on the interface in the-condenser.
The principal fluid dynamic problems in these systems are the avoid-

ance of liquid carryover from the evaporator to the turbine and of vapor
carryover from the condenser to the pump inlet.

In a normal gravity or

equivalent acceleration environment these problems are avoided by proper
elevation and orientation of the various components, In a weightless environment where the stabilizing effects of buoyancy are absent, however,
liquid and vapor carryover may become difficult to avoid.

The situation

may be aggrevated by random acceleration from attitude tbrustors, drag,
crew movements, etc, Further complications will come from the l o s s of obvious positional stability between tubes of evaporators and condensers employing multiple tubes and from possible changes in heat-transfer mechanisms
in the tubes.

these. systems, one i n t h e owgen supply tank and one i n the water receiver

A t h i r d liquid-vapor i n t e r f a c e i s found i n t h e water separator.

The problems i n t h e tank systems are s i m i l a r t o those encountered i n
t h e main propellant tank except t h a t here t h e principal problern i s t o produce o r vent vapor from the tank without l3ss of liquid.

I n the case of

the oxygen supply tank t h i s vapor i s generated by t h e addition of heat,
and any changes i n heat-transfer mechanisms w i l l become important as i n
t h e propellant tank system and i n the evaporator o f t h e space power system.
I n addition, ljecause t h e heat must be added t o t h e l i q u i d i n order t o

.

generate vapor, t h e configuration, locatlon, and dynamic behavlor o f t h e
l i q u i d will be important i n desig@ng t h e heating system.

The accelera-

t i o n perturbations imposed by a t t i t t d e thrusteys, drag, crew movements,
etc, w i l l muse movements of t h e l i q u i d i n each tank similar t o t h a t i n
t h e main propellant tank.

The separation of water or water vapor from a moving gas stream i s
nsua1l.y accomplished by impingement of t h e a i r stream against a cooled
surface, whfch condenses t h e water vapor.
t o t h e receiver,

The water then drains by gravity

I n a weightless environment t h e drainfng action i s l o s t

an3 other means must be found t o remove t h e water from t h e condensing surface.

3.1

RESEARCH EFFORTS

Discussion of Analytical Concepts

I n studying the change i n f l u i d behavior o r b a s i c mechanisms between l - g and 0-g, one i s l e d t o examine t h e change i n forces or force
levels i n going from one gravity f i e l d t o t h e other.

I n general, t h e

forces acting on a l i q u i d are t h e i n e r t i a l forces and t h e intermolecular
forces, which are exhibited i n the form of t h e surface-tension forces.
Under 1-g o r earthbound conditions a l i q u i d i n a container i s restrained
from ac'celerating f r e e l y i n t h e gravity f i e l d .

TTnder these condPtions

the i n e r t i a l Forces predominate, and only small e f f e c t s of t h e surface-

tension forces are noticeable.

Typically, the e f f e c t s of dominant i n e r t i a l

forces a r e t h e shaping of l i q u i d s i n ta-nks, t h e weight of the liquid, the

-

h y Q o s t a t i c pressure of' l i q u i d columns, t h e rise of vapor bubbles i n l i q u i d s
due t o buoyancy, and t h e convection currents i n l i q u i d s as a r e s u l t of
heat inputs.

Although t h e surface-tension forces are s m a l l i n comparison

with i n e r t i a forces a t 1-g, t h e i r e f f e c t s are s t i l l observable i n t h e
meniscus o f liquids i n contact with s o l i d bodies and i n the c a p i l l a r y r i s e

o r depression i n small diameter tubes,
When a l i q u i d or a system containing l i q u i d and vapor i s allowed t o
accelerate f r e e l y i n t h e l o c a l gravity f i e l d , t h e i n e r t i a forces disappear
and the surface-tension forces remain.

Early History of Surface-Ehergy Studies

The history of t h e study of surface-tension phenomena can be traced

as far back as Leonard0 da Vinci and S i r Isaac Newton.

It was Young,

however, who first established t h e theory by demonstrating how the principles of surface tension and contact angle can be used t o explain a great
many c a p i l l a r y phenomena.

The theory was put on a firm mathematical

foundation by Laplace and later Poisson.

Further developments were made

by Gauss, who applied the principle of conservation of e n e r a t o t h e sys-

tem and obtained not only t h e equation of the free surface but a l s o t h e
conditions of t h e contact an@e,

Other earlier investigators include

Eupre, Rayleigh, Gibbs, and Plateau, who x t i l i z e d soap bubbles and other de-

.'

'

v i c e s . t o deteimine t h e equilibrium configuration of liquids,

Later in-

vestigations were c a r r i e d out by Langmuir and Harkins i n the United States
and by Adam md Burdon i n England

kraporation and Condensation

The f r e e liquid-vapor interfaces i n evaporators and condensers have
not contributed t o operational and performance problems as they 'nave i n
the tanks of other systems.

This is due t o the s m a l l diameter of t.ubes

used i n eva-pot-ators and condensers (usually l e s s than 1/2 i n . ) , which
according t o the Bond number c r i t e r i o n rnakes them st.able t o accelerations
of 0.03 g o r more f o r t h e smaller tubes (0.1 g f o r 5/16-in. tube).

Accel-

e r a t i o n perturbations of f h i s magnitude wiLl not o r d i n a r i l y be i n the
operating spectrum of t h e vehicle except when under boost thrus+,, which

will permit normal operation of t h e system.
32 3

Because forced convection will exist i n evaporators and condensers
designed f o r minimum weight , it is unlikely t h a t there- will be any appreciable change i n t h e heat-transfer mechanism.

The p o s s i b i l i t y of genera-

t i o n of bubbles within t h e l i q u i d i n t h e evaporator o r t h e inclusion of
vapor i n t h e l i q u i d i n t h e condenser was o r i g i n a l l y of some concern; how-

ever, t h e reasonably large s t a b i l i t y of t h e interface and t h e use of
counterflow heat exchangers t o provide subcooling below t h e interface and
a temperature gradient t o rove bubbles towal-d t h e interface has v i r t u a l l y

eliminated these problems.

and increase t h e separation s t a b i l i t y margin.

duced i n t o two-phase pressure-drop correlations f o r use a t zero gravity.

I n h i s analysis, surface tension e f f e c t s a r e intrdduced by assuming t h a t
t h e f r i c t i o n drag i s proportional t o t h e s i z e of condensed droplets adhering t o the wall, which increases t h e tube roughness.

The assumption

of w a l l bound droDlets i s i n keeping with t h e tendency for t h e surface
energy t o minimize.
Most of t h e a c t u a l zero-gravity data obtained t o date has been concerned with observation of droplet and i n t e r f a c e behavior and o n l y q u a l i t a t i v e measurement of t h e pressure drop.

CONCLUDING REMARXS

This paper has reviewed the a n a l y t i c a l and experimental research
directed toward the solution of the pro-blems encountered with liquidvapor systems i n weightlessness and has presented the r e s u l t s of some recent experimental work studying t h e correlation of certain aspects of t h e
dynamic behavior of t h e interface with t h e applicable dimensionless scalThe various problems encountered i n the liquid-vapor sys-

ing parameters,

tems were grouped i n t o the categories of interface configuration, i n t e r face dynamics, pool t o i l h g heat-transfer mechanisms, and evaporation and
/

cmdensat ion phenomena.

The greatest portion of weightlessness research up t o t h i s t i m e has
been devoted t o studies of the parameters defining t h e c o d i g u r a t i o n and
position of t h e liquid-vapor interface under t h e conditions of no external
disturbances,

The t h e o r e t i c a l background i s now extensive and has been

verified by experiments,

'

A t the present t i m e most of the work i n t h i s

category is more of a development nature devoted t o t h e applfcation of t h e
principles t o systems t o provide arid maintain proper separation and
orientation of l i q u i d and vapor.
With the b e t t e r understanding of t h e configuration and position of the
interface, more a t t e a t i o x has recently been given t o t h e dynamic behavior
of the interface.

It has been found t h a t a great deal of t h e o r e t i c a l work

dealing with f l u i d dynamics i s applicable t o zero-gravity interface behavior.

A sizeable amount of experimental work verifying the theory has

been performed, but a great deal (both analytical. and experimental) remains
t o be done--especialiy t h a t applicable t o interface dynamics i n response

tg

32 7

acceleration perturbations ( a t t i t u d e controls, docking j o l t s , etc.

and

outflow disturbances.
A great deal of i n t e r e s t has been shown i n t h e change i n the heat-

t r a n s f e r lnechanism with reduction in g r a v i t y or acceleration level.

The

r e s u l t i n g studies have approached t h e problem i n several ways by studylng
the fundamental bubble mechanics, by measuring the heat flux as a f'unct i o n of d i f f e r e n t i a l temperature input, and by measuring the pressure
rise i n a closed system.,

Attempts have been made t o correlate t h e re-

s u l t s with e x i s t i n g heat-transfer theory.

Although some understanding
I

has been gained, a great deal of work remains i n the a r e a o f understanding

f'undamental bubble mechanics.
Solutions f o r some of t h e evaporator and condenser liquid-vapor i n t e r face problems have become evident from the interface configuration 'and
'dynamics studies

.

The most work a t the' present t i m e i s concentrated on

studies of t h e pressure drop i n condensers because it i s important t o t h e
efficiency of t h e system and t o t h e tube-to-tube s t a b i l i t y .
experimental r e s u l t s a r e available a t t h e present t i m e .

ZERO GRAvrrY LIQUID PROPELLANT CONTROL

BY DIELECTROPRORESIS
J. B. BlackmoM
Douglas A i r c r a f t Company, Inc., Santa Monica, California

A method of c o l l e c t i n g l i q u i d propellants i n zero g r a v i t y by use of e l e c t r i c fields
i s presented.

The method u t i l i z e s t h e concept of dielectrophoresis, which i s de-

fined as t h e induced motion of e l e c t r i c a l l y n e u t r a l bodies i n a non-uniform e l e c t r i l
field.

A b r i e f analysis of t h e f i e l d forces i s presented, and l i q u i d c o l l e c t i o n

experiments i n simulated and a c t u a l zero g r a v i t y environments a r e described, i n cluciing zero-g t e s t s iri a i r c r a f t flying b a l l i s t i c t r a j e c t o r i e s

.,

A representative dielectrophoretic propeliant c o l l e c t i o n system i s described for a
250,000 l b l i q u i d hydrogen-liquid oxygen space vehicle.

The system i s designed t o

O'.
c o l l e c t propellant with average forces on t h e order of l-g

The system wei.ghs

app.roxim?tely 400 l b s and can be operated continuously i n zero g r a v i t y by use of

solar c e l l s as t h e primary power source.

I n addition, it i s shown t h a t propellants

such 8s l i q u i a hydrogen, can be collected o f f of t h e t a n k walls and i n t o t h e center

of t h e tar& t o provide a n i n s u l a t i n g gas l a y e r which.decreases the w a l l t o propellarit heat t m n s f e r and nence boil-oi'f of t h e pro2ellant.

Such e. system could de-

crease the b o i l - o f f of t h e l i q u i d hydrogen from 3300 l b s t o approximately 800 fbs.

Including t h e systen: weight, t h e t o t a l weight savings would be epproximately 2100 1
The use of high voltages wlth space vehicles is discussed, including descriptions

of the most Promising high voltage generator, e l e c t r i c a l breakdown control, znd.
electrode designs which minimize t.he danger of a r c s i n t h e cxidizer tanks.

The e f f e c t s of e l e c t r i c f i e l d s on d i e l e c t r i c l i q u i d s are described below, and t h e

equations required f o r designing a dielectrophoretic collection system are presented.
A continuous, d i e l e c t r i c l i q u i d i n a non-uniform e l e c t r i c f i e l d experience.s forces

r'esulting from f r e e charges and from gradients i n t h e f i e l d i n t e n s i t y and l i q u i d
d i e l e c t r i c constant.

exerted due t o inhomogenity of t h e liquid, and t h e last term denotes %he dielectrophoretic e f f e c t of force on a body i n a non-uniform e l e c t r i c f i e l d .

The last term

' i s a l s o known as the e l e c t r o s t r i c t i o n force.

Although capillary forces w i l l aid collection

i n nearly a l l electrode designs, the effect is usually small compared t o the dielec3rophoretic forces.

I n addition, capillary effects result fo surface forces,
rm

and, because they are essentially passive, a r e determined o n l y by the design of

A capillary system has the disadvantage that

vapor bubbles generated within the collected liquid w i l l not be forced out.

Also,

surface tension decreases as temperature increases and thus collection forces w i l l
be decreased i n areas of high heat transfer such as a r e l i k e l y st the bottom of

propellant tanks close t o the rocket engines.

359

Dielectrcsphoretic forces, huwever, are body forces and can be controlled by varying.
t h e magnitude of t h e applied voltage or by varying t h e e l e c t r i c f i e l d configuratXon
used f o r c o l l e c t i o n o r venting, i.e.,

by charging or discharging selected electrodes

Furthermore, t h e dielectrophoretic f o r c e increases as t h e temperature of t h e l i q u i d

decreases because of t h e temperature dependence of t h e d i e l e c t r i c constant.

Thus,

free convection currents w i l l be set up within a l i q u i d with variable temperature
i n zero g r a v i t y which w i l l a t t r a c t t h e colder l i q u i d i n t o areas of higher f i e l d
intensity.’

Any vapor bubbles formed w i l l be displaced by the a t t r a c t e d l i q u i d .

HIGH VOLTAGE PROBLENS AND C N R L
OTO

Some of the most serious problems associated with a dielectrophoretic systen irlvolve the generation and c o n t r o l of t h e high voltages reqcired for l a r g e s-,ace
Voltages on t h e order of 105-10 5 v o l t s are required with l a r g e
tanks to minimize t h e system weight. I n p r i n c i p l e , t h e e l e c t r i c f i e l d i n t e n s i t i e s

vehicle tanks.

. required f o r c o l l e c t i o n forces on th" order of

g eould be obtained with l o w

voltages and small separation distances between electrodes.

However, t h e .number

of electrodes required and t h e electrode weight would then be prohibitive.
The major problems which arise because of t h e high voltage requirement are (I)
t h e generation of high a c voltages, and { 2 ) %he c o n t r o l of e l e n k r i c a l b r e a k d o m ,

especially i n oxidizer tanks.
Various high voltage e l e c t r o s t a t i c generators have been discussed i n Reference 8
which appear s u i t a b l e f o r a dielectrophoretic system.

A t t h i s point, t h e most

promising type is a Van de Graaff generator, b u i l t by Ion Physics Corp.,
supplies 200 L* amperes a t 600,000 v o l t s . 14,' 5 ,
J

which

m generator i s approximately
e

8 inches i n diameter and 12 inches i n length and can be operated a t an ac frequency
of 1 cycle/sec with large tanks.

Since t h i s frequency i s much g r e a t e r than t h e

371

reciprocal of t h e relaxation time of such propellants as LH2, LO2, etc., the
device is s u i t a b l e f o r advanced space vehicle applications.

The power output

is orders of magnitude above t h e resistive power loss i n t h e propellants and
t h e generator weight for a f l i g h t application would be on t h e order of 15-20

lbs.

Similar devices can be developed for p a r t i c u l a r applications.

The

Van de Graaff generator operated i n an a c mode i s a l s o s u i t a b l e fo t h e
rm
standpoints of lifetime, s t r u c t u r a l strength, and r e l i a b i l i t y .
The e l e c t r i c a l breakdown problem i s s i g n i f i c a n t f o r two reasons.

F i r s t , arc-

ing or e l e c t r i c a l breakdown i n the tank destroys t h e e l e c t r i c f i e l d and hence
t h e collecting force.

Second, a r c s i n an oxidizer tank s t r i k i n g a metallic

surface could cause i g n i t i o n an6 destroy t h e vehicle.

Therefore, a dielec-

trophoretic system must .be designed t o minimize t h e occurrence of arcing,
. and

to-guarantee t h a t a r c s i n an Qxidizer tank w i l l ' n o t i n i t i a t e combusklon

of the electrodes o r tank walls.

Arcing can be eliminated by.proper electrode

and insulation design, monitoring of t h e current and t h e use of sui+,able voltage safety factors.
I n general, the electrodes should be designed such t h a t there &re no regions

where the e l e c t r i c f i e l d i n t e n s i t y i s much greater than on the major portion
of the electrodes.

For instance, with a cylindrical electrode having

8

hemispherical cap, t h e f i e l d i n t e n s i t y of the cap i s much greater than along
t h e length of t h e cylinder.

Therefore, t h e cap radius should be increased

(and joined smoothly t o t h e cylinder) such that t h e e l e c t r i c f i e l d i n t e n s i t y
i s approximatelythe same f o r t h e e n t i r e electrode surface.

t h e t o t a l e l e c t r i c f i e l d energy and collecting force.

This maximizes

However, i n some

applications higher f i e l d i n t e n s i t i e s and hence collecting forces may be

required i n c e r t a i n areas t o cause p r e f e r e n t i a l location, as a t t h e bottom
of t h e tank near t h e pump o u t l e t .

I n a l l cases, electrode surfaces should be

polished t o remove a s p e r i t i e s which cause l o c a l corona discharge.
Since the d i e l e c t r i c s t r e n g t h of t h e propellant vapor and pressurizing gas is
. l e s s than that of t h e liquid,. t h e electrodes must be designed f o r voltages
w e l l below t h e g a s breakdown voltage.

The voltage s a f e t y factor ( r a t i o of

corona discharge voltage t o applied voltage) f o r breakdown i n t h e gas phase
i s approximately 3 and 10 f o r dielectrophoretic systems designed t o c o l l e c t

l i q u i d oxygen and l i q u i d hydrogen, respectively, w i t h average forces on the
order of 10-3g.
nitude higher.

The voltage s a f e t y f a c t o r i n t h e l i q u i d i s an order of magAlso, i n zero gravity, t h e l i q u i d i s collected t o t h e electrode

surfaces, where t h e f i e l d i n t e n s i t y is g r e a t e s t , with forces on t h e order.of
1 g.

Therefore, t h e r e i s an additional s a f e t y f a c t o r i n h v i n g the high

d i e l e c t r i c strength l i q u i d coqtinuously collected on t h e electrode s,urfaces
even with very violent disturbing forces caused by a t t i t u d e control, docking
of the vehicle, etc.
The p o s s i b i l i t y of arcing i s also minimized by monitoring t h e current between
electrodes.

Because most electrode designs have very highly non-uniform

e l e c t r i c fields, corona discharge w i l l occur a t electrode s u r f a c e s - a t voltages

w e l l below t h e arcing voltages.

Onset of corona discharge can be monitored

by measuring t h e t y p i c a l exponential current increase, and t h e voltage can
then be automatically lowered below t h e corona onset voltage.

The response

t i m e t o lower t h e voltage would be less than t h e time f o r a voltage cycle.

Corona discharge or arcing i n t h e f u e l tank (such as

9 presents
)

s i n c e there i s no oxidizer present t o support combustion.

no danger

However, i n an

3 73

oxidizer tank (such as LO2) t h e danger of arc-initiaked combustion of metallic
electrodes is p a r t i c u l a r l y acute.

The problem of electrode i g n i t i o n can be

solved by constructing t h e electrodes of a r e l a t i v e l y i n e r t non-combustible
d i e l e c t r i c material such as t e f l o n o r mylar with a t h i n film metallic coating
sandwiched between the d i e l e c t r i c s .
surface f o r t h e high voltage.

The metallic coating is t h e conducting

The energy of any a r c s s t r i k i n g t h e electrode

would be absorbed by t h e d i e l e c t r i c material.

If an a r c penetrated t h e dielec-

t r i c and ignited t h e t h i n f i l m coating, combustion would be prevented because
t h e metallic deposit would be insulated from the oxidizer.

Even i f t h e corn-

p l e t e metallic coating were t o i g n i t e the t o t a l energy produced would be
negligible because of the small amounts of metal involved.

The p o s s i b i l i t y of arcs s t r i k i n g t h e oxidizer tank w a l l can be minimized by
designikg the electrodes .such t h a t t h e *regions of maximum f i e l d i n t e n s i t ?
a r e between t h e electrodes and not between the electrodes and t h e tank wall.
Any arcs which might occur would then pass from one electrode t o another,

rather than from a n electrode t o t h e tank w a l l .

I n addition, l i q u i d oxygen

tank walls often are i n t e r n a l l y ins&ated with materials which are s u i t a b l e
d i e l e c t r i c s and compatible w i t h the l i q u i d oxygen.

Arcs yhich might occur

between t h e electrodes and tank w a l l would then d i s s i p a t e energy i n penetrating the insulation.

The arcing problem i s serious but appears solvable because of t h e combined
solutions of high voltage s a f e t y factors, voltage control by corona onset
monitoring, and proper electrode design.

3 74

The dielectrophoretic propellant c o l l e c t i o n system can c o l l e c t l i q u i d propell a n t i n zero g r a v i t y with average forces on t h e order of
system weight and power requirements are low.

I n addition, the system can

be used t o decrease propellant b o i l - o f f , e s p e c i a l l y with l i q u i d hydrogen.

The system appears most promising f o r space vehicles requiring five-ten or
more engine restarts o r u l l a g e gas venting cycles, o r f o r long term storage
of cryogenic l i q u i d with minimm boil-off.

The generation of t h e high -Joltages

required for l a r g e vehicles i s r e l a t i v e l y easy t o achieve with currei?tly available, l i g h t weight Van de Graaff gecerators.

The problem of voltage control

and arcing appears solvable.

I n view o f t h e advantages offered by a dielectrophoretic system, it i s de. s i r a b l e t o consider use of t h i s approaeh i n space veh'icles an$ to'bear i n
mind the p o s s i b l e a p p l i c a t i o n of t h e concept i n o t h e r areas.
